In an ablation, ignoring execution-induced observation loss increases aggregate mission success and reverses the ordering of two runtime strategies. Replays using Jetson AGX Orin CPU and GPU profiles change the relative success of local and remote strategies. In selected Jetson GPU-profile replays, intent-driven adaptation reduces modeled task energy by up to 28% with unchanged mission success. EmbodiedIntentBench provides an extensible framework for evaluating runtime decisions against mission fulfillment under physical and resource constraints.</p></details> | <details><summary>Paper...</summary><p>Paper is currently under review. Authors' version posted for personal use and not for redistribution</p></details> |
| **[Grip, Trace, Write: Force-Aware Imitation and Reinforcement Learning for High-DoF Robotic Handwriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10451v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Most robotic handwriting rigidly mounts the pen and reduces writing to trajectory tracking. A freely held pen instead couples grasp stability, tip motion, and contact force. We present Grip, Trace, Write, a force-aware simulation environment and learning framework for high-DoF handwriting. A two-branch actor separates pen retention from arm-wrist writing while preserving their closed-loop coupling. Training learns a grasp controller, initializes writing from inverse-kinematics demonstrations by behavior cloning, and improves writing through reinforcement learning with rewards on ink, force, time, and retention. Across diverse tasks, the policy improves completion, precision, force regulation, and efficiency over behavior cloning and inverse kinematics, and generalizes without fine-tuning to unseen English and Chinese content. These results establish force-aware handwriting as a testbed for dexterous tool use.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This paper is accepted by CoRL 2026 Workshop PhysWM</p></details> |
| **[Multi-Objective Adaptation After Deployment: A Position on World Models for Remote Robot Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10435v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous robot teams deployed in remote, unstructured environments may face changes in the environment, hardware, and team composition that degrade previously effective policies. Shifting mission objectives can be handled by selecting a different solution from an existing Pareto set, but shifting deployment conditions can reorder the set itself, making previously dominated policies competitive or new policies necessary. However, evaluating these policies in new contexts while minimizing risk to the hardware remains an open problem. We argue that, although largely untested in this domain, world models merit investigation for post-deployment multi-objective adaptation. By predicting policy outcomes under updated estimates of current conditions, world models could enable teams to re-evaluate existing policies without physically executing each candidate, provided predicted performance changes reflect those realized on deployed systems. We identify research priorities for model-based policy re-evaluation, validation of predicted against realized performance, and incorporating shared information into world models to support coordinated team adaptation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Detecting and Explaining Robot Policy Failures from Sparse Internal Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10400v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visuomotor policies can handle many robot manipulation tasks, but understanding their failures remains difficult. Unfamiliar conditions and small action errors can lead to failure, yet watching the robot alone tells us little about what went wrong inside the policy. We introduce MIND (Mechanistic Interpretability for Neural Diagnosis), a method that looks inside a trained policy to detect failures during execution and identify the internal concepts involved. MIND uses a sparse autoencoder to break down the policy's internal activations into features that can be studied individually. We give meaning to these features by linking their activity to the robot's position, movement, and surroundings, and by steering their activations to examine how they affect the robot's actions. From successful and failed executions, we learn how the features behave on the way to a failure. During execution, MIND flags deviations from these patterns and uses the meaningful features contributing most to the change to describe the problem. Experiments in simulation and on a physical robot show that changes in these internal features help detect and explain failures. By using the same features for detection and diagnosis, MIND connects failure signals to understandable changes inside the policy.</p></details> |  |
| **[GUARD: Anomaly-Informed Q-Steering for Generative Robot Policies in Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10343v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative robot policies based on diffusion and flow matching can model complex, multimodal behaviors, but remain susceptible to covariate shift during closed-loop execution. Existing approaches mitigate such failures through additional demonstrations, policy fine-tuning, or test-time guidance, but often require human intervention, engineered rewards, or other task-specific signals. We introduce GUARD, an anomaly-informed Q-steering framework that leverages the policy's training distribution to construct a dense, failure-anticipatory steering signal. GUARD pairs sparse task rewards with dense anomaly scores learned from policy training data and propagates both through a learned Q-function. Its gradients then steer the denoising process toward actions that improve task return while preventing trajectories from entering poorly supported regions, without directly modifying the pretrained policy. Across four Adroit dexterous manipulation tasks, GUARD achieves average success rates of $96.0\%$ and $80.0\%$ under nominal and perturbed dynamics, outperforming the strongest baseline by $21.0$ and $20.0$ percentage points while maintaining the lowest anomaly score. GUARD further demonstrates direct sim-to-real transfer on in-hand screwdriver rotation and reorientation tasks, achieving $40$ and $25$ percentage-point gains in success rate. These results demonstrate that anomaly signals can serve not only for failure monitoring but also to complement sparse task rewards as effective supervision for robust policy steering.</p></details> | 8 pages, 4 figures |
| **[The World Model Planning Accelerator (WMPA): Hardware-Algorithm Co-Design for Embedded AI in Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10316v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embedded AI is moving from perception to prediction. Robots now use world models$\unicode{x2014}$learned simulators that predict the outcome of candidate actions in a compressed latent space before any is executed. Every predicted step must be computed on board within a tight power and thermal budget, yet the energy these steps fundamentally require, and how far real hardware sits from it, have not been characterized. We derive lower bounds on the arithmetic operations and the CMOS dynamic energy of a latent state transition from information theory and Boolean circuit complexity. Profiling a DreamerV3-style Recurrent State Space Model (RSSM) shows that every rollout operation is memory-bandwidth bound and that a GPU consumes at least $5.9\times10^{10}$ times the CMOS energy floor. Transient 3D thermal simulations show that this inefficiency is also a thermal one, since sustained back-to-back planning heats a millimeter-scale silicon tile to 556 K even behind an ideal copper heat spreader. Guided by this chain from bound to bottleneck, we propose the World Model Planning Accelerator (WMPA), a chip architecture for embedded world model planning that combines near-memory computation, real-time entropy estimation, variable-precision rollout units, and copy-on-write latent scratchpads. With a design target of 40x lower power, the same tile stays near ambient at 306 K in simulation, pointing toward practical planning on thermally constrained robots.</p></details> | 7 pages, 4 figures |
| **[Mobility Information Capacity of Multi-Robot Systems: Safety-Aware Maneuver Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10298v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multiple robots operating in shared workspaces must adjust their speed, direction, and trajectories to coordinate with one another and respond to changing tasks and disturbances. The available maneuver freedom depends on the dynamics, uncertainty, and safety margins, even for a fixed fleet size and workspace. However, robot count, workspace occupancy, and task throughput do not quantify how much the fleet can vary its motion or how reliably these variations can be observed. We develop a mobility information capacity (MIC) framework that jointly characterizes these two aspects. Mobility information is defined as the mutual information between a joint maneuver sequence and its noisy trajectory observation, and MIC maximizes this quantity under maneuver-resource constraints. For a linear Gaussian maneuver-to-observation model with feedback dynamics, we characterize the finite-horizon information benchmark and derive its stationary long-horizon spectral limit, accounting for heterogeneous mobility gains, colored uncertainty, and temporally coupled maneuver costs. We further map workspace-boundary, inter-robot separation, and actuator chance constraints into coupled maneuver-covariance restrictions and derive conditions under which covariance optimization reduces to resource allocation across mobility modes. Finite joint maneuver codebooks are then constructed through individual risk screening and noise-whitened trajectory separation, with bounds linking sequence recovery error and achieved information to the capacity benchmarks. An aerial-robot simulation study evaluates spectral convergence, safety-aware allocation, finite-codebook identification, and fleet-size scaling. The resulting framework complements occupancy and task-throughput measures by quantifying the maximum observable maneuver freedom supported by a mobility system.</p></details> | <details><summary>12-pa...</summary><p>12-page main article and 4-page supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[Who Taught the Robot That? Removing One User's Corrections from a Robot Policy That Learns Online](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10280v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot that keeps learning after deployment is corrected by several people in turn, and one of them may later ask to be removed. We show that whether the robot can forget that person depends on how people correct it. The goal of removal is the robot that would exist if the person had never been there, which we compute exactly in two simulated tasks with scripted users. When users correct the robot by taking over control, an ordered log of training updates is enough: retraining on the remaining sessions in their original order gives a robot as close to the goal as training noise allows. Deleting the person's data and retraining in one pass does not, even when the other users' corrections do not depend on the removed person, because it loses the order of training. When users only nudge the robot's action, the other users' corrections carry the removed person's influence, and no method we tested recovers the goal robot. Continuing to train without the person removes most of their habits, but not what their data in the training buffer keep alive. The log can identify one clearly harmful user, but not rank users by influence better than counting their corrections.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the HumanRLI Workshop at CoRL 2026</p></details> |
| **[Agibot: An Open-Source Modular Swerve-Drive Robot Platform for Agricultural Robotics Research and Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10235v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agricultural production faces persistent labor shortages and increasing demand for automation, yet progress in agricultural robotics remains constrained by the limited availability of affordable, flexible, and accessible research platforms. This paper presents Agibot, an open-source, modular, low-cost agricultural robotics platform designed for autonomy research and experimentation, with potential applications in hands-on robotics education. The platform integrates four-wheel independent swerve drive, swappable computing, distributed embedded control, multimodal sensing, and configurable manipulation within a unified sense-plan-act-verify framework supporting perception, control, navigation, manipulation, and autonomous task verification. The platform was evaluated through representative row-crop navigation and manipulation tasks under varying lighting and soil conditions. Experiments demonstrated reliable lane following, autonomous row-to-row navigation, target identification, seed dispensing, plant manipulation, and closed-loop verification of task success. Its modular architecture supports independent testing, modification, and validation of autonomy components for rapid prototyping and experimentation. These proof-of-concept tasks, controller-tuning studies, and integrated trials demonstrate the platform's research capabilities, while its accessible and reconfigurable design supports its use for hands-on robotics education. By combining affordability, modularity, open-source accessibility, configurable manipulation, and end-to-end sense-plan-act-verify autonomy, Agibot provides a practical platform for agricultural robotics research, technology development, and workforce training.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages. Submitted to IEEE Transactions on AgriFood Electronics (TAFE)</p></details> |
| **[SyncTwin: Fast Digital Twin Construction and Synchronization for Safe Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09920v3)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate and safe robotic manipulation under dynamic and visually occluded conditions remains a core challenge in real-world deployment. We introduce SyncTwin, a novel digital twin framework that unifies fast 3D scene reconstruction and real-to-sim synchronization for robust and safety-aware robotic manipulation in such environments. In the offline stage, we employ VGGT to rapidly reconstruct object-level 3D assets from RGB images, forming a reusable geometry library. During execution, SyncTwin continuously synchronizes the digital twin by tracking real-world object states and uses stored assets to complete partially observed geometry. The synchronized twin enables motion planners to compute collision-aware robot trajectories in simulation, with execution on the real robot closing the real-to-sim-to-real loop. Experiments in dynamic and occluded scenes show that SyncTwin improves manipulation performance and motion safety, demonstrating the effectiveness of digital twin synchronization for real-world robotic execution.</p></details> | <details><summary>IEEE ...</summary><p>IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters; Project Website: https://sync-twin.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Locating Contact on Tendon-Driven Continuum Robots From Tendon Measurements Alone via Constrained Moving Horizon Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09877v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tendon-driven continuum robots can reach through constrained spaces, but contact along their bodies is invisible to the sensors at their motors. We show that this is a structural limitation rather than a tuning problem: the map from contact forces to tendon measurements has a null space of dimension at least seven, so no estimator that leaves the contact support free can locate contact. When the obstacle geometry is known, however, non-penetration and complementarity make the contact force locally identifiable, and we prove that the resulting system with unknown contact inputs is locally exponentially strongly detectable. We present a constrained moving horizon estimator (MHE) that encodes these conditions and runs in real time as one quadratic program per sample, and we give a local, mode-wise robust stability result for it. In a finite- element benchmark of a two-section robot pressing and sliding on a wall, the estimator located contact to 0.9 mm and estimated contact force to 0.22 N, compared with 65 mm and 0.72 N for a tuned extended Kalman filter (EKF) with state projection. The estimator tolerated millimeter- level errors in the obstacle position and unmodeled wall friction, under which the projected EKF failed. Under randomized model mismatch over 40 trials, including unmodeled tendon friction, it remained better in force and location in every trial. Without contact, the MHE offered no advantage over the EKF, which confirms that its benefit comes from the contact constraints. This report extends a letter submitted to IEEE Control Systems Letters with complete proofs and additional experiments.</p></details> |  |
| **[Autonomous Whole-body Loco-manipulation Using a Snake Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09777v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Snake robots can leverage their hyper-redundant morphology for both locomotion and manipulation through distributed body contact but whole-body loco-manipulation is particularly challenging since the entire body serves simultaneously as propulsive mechanism and manipulation tool Prior work on wheeled legged and snake robot platforms has decoupled these functions through dedicated appendages body segmentation or grippers We present closed-loop loco-manipulation with our snake robot COBRA without this distinction such that any part of the body may make contact We develop a contact-implicit dynamic model unifying robot-ground robot-object and object-ground interactions within a Moreau-Jean time-stepping framework and formulate a trajectory optimization over the full-order dynamics guided by a geometric planner and nominal proportional controller to autonomously push an object to a goal location during sidewinding We validate the planner and controller on hardware across varied configurations and present simulation results for a simplified trajectory optimization that resolves robot-object and object-ground contacts</p></details> | <details><summary>Prese...</summary><p>Presented in IROS 2026</p></details> |
| **[5G-NetBotSim: A Synchronized Co-Simulation Framework for Connected Robots over ROS and srsRAN-based 5G Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09735v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Connected robotic applications increasingly rely on wireless communication for teleoperation, coordination, and distributed autonomy. However, robotic simulators often use ideal links or abstract delay models that do not capture cellular scheduling and protocol processing effects. We present 5G NetBotSim, a realtime co simulation framework integrating ROS/Gazebo workloads with an executable, full stack srsRAN 5G network emulator. The framework provides isolated User Equipment (UE) robot contexts and stage level timing across the host, gNB, UE, ROS, and robot execution stages. We evaluate human to robot teleoperation and robot to robot leader follower coordination under controlled channel quality conditions. Results show how CQI and MCS variations propagate to throughput, communication latency, and robot performance. In leader follower coordination, higher channel quality reduces mean tracking error from 0.558 m to 0.460 m, a 17.6% reduction. These results demonstrate protocol stack in the loop co simulation for joint network and robotic-application evaluation.</p></details> |  |
